    The cost function. Bundle adjustment (BA) jointly refines all camera parameters $\{\mathbf{K}_i,\mathbf{R}_i,\mathbf{t}_i\}$ and 3D points $\{\mathbf{X}_j\}$ to minimise reprojection error: $$\min_{\{\mathbf{P}_i\},\{\mathbf{X}_j\}} \sum_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{O}} \rho\!\left(\big\|\pi(\mathbf{P}_i,\mathbf{X}_j) - \mathbf{x}_{ij}\big\|^2_{\Sigma_{ij}}\right)$$ where $\pi$ is the full projection (including distortion), $\mathcal{O}$ the set of observations, $\Sigma_{ij}$ the measurement covariance, and $\rho$ a robust loss (Huber, Cauchy) that down-weights outliers. "Bundle" refers to the bundle of rays through each camera centre. The classic reference is Triggs et al.'s survey [199].

    Levenberg–Marquardt (LM). Linearise residuals $\mathbf{r}(\boldsymbol{\theta}+\boldsymbol{\delta}) \approx \mathbf{r} + \mathbf{J}\boldsymbol{\delta}$ and solve $$(\mathbf{J}^\top\mathbf{J} + \mu\,\mathbf{D})\,\boldsymbol{\delta} = -\mathbf{J}^\top\mathbf{r}.$$ Small $\mu$ ≈ Gauss–Newton (fast near the optimum); large $\mu$ ≈ gradient descent (safe far away). $\mu$ is adapted per step depending on whether the cost decreased. For ML readers: this is a second-order trust-region method on a sum of squares, not SGD. It converges in tens of iterations, but only to the local minimum near the initialisation, which is why SfM spends so much effort on good initial poses.

    Sparsity and the Schur complement. Each residual depends on one camera and one point, so $\mathbf{J}^\top\mathbf{J}$ has a block "arrowhead" structure: a large block-diagonal point block $\mathbf{V}$, a smaller camera block $\mathbf{U}$, and off-diagonal blocks $\mathbf{W}$. Eliminating points gives the reduced camera system $(\mathbf{U} - \mathbf{W}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{W}^\top)\boldsymbol{\delta}_c = \dots$, whose size is set by the number of cameras, not points. Ceres Solver (used by COLMAP) exploits this; COLMAP 4.1.0 also added a GPU BA backend, Caspar, which its changelog says is often 1–2 orders of magnitude faster than the Ceres CUDA backend on medium-to-large problems [23].

    Gauge freedom. Reprojection error is unchanged if you apply any similarity transform $\mathbf{S}\in\mathrm{Sim}(3)$ (rotation, translation, uniform scale: 7 DoF) to all cameras and points together. The normal equations are therefore rank-deficient by 7. Solvers fix the gauge by holding one camera fixed and the scale (e.g. one baseline) fixed, or by damping. Practically: BA alone can never tell you where the origin is, which way is up, or how big anything is. Those come from external information (GCPs, IMU gravity, known distances).

    2.1.6The scale ambiguity of monocular reconstruction, and Sim(3) alignment#

    Multiply every 3D point and every camera translation by $s>0$: all projections are unchanged. A single moving camera therefore reconstructs the scene up to an unknown global scale, and in long sequences, scale can also drift (different parts of the trajectory end up at slightly different scales) unless loops are closed or constraints added. Visual-inertial systems recover metric scale from accelerometer data; stereo rigs from a known baseline; our scenario has no GNSS, and although the .OSV metadata carries fused attitude quaternions (a gravity prior, Ch. 1), no metric accelerometer stream suitable for visual-inertial scale is available, so scale must come from measured markers or known dimensions.

    Sim(3) alignment finds the similarity $(s,\mathbf{R},\mathbf{t})$ that best maps reconstructed points $\mathbf{p}_k$ to reference points $\mathbf{q}_k$: $$\min_{s,\mathbf{R},\mathbf{t}} \sum_k \|\mathbf{q}_k - (s\mathbf{R}\mathbf{p}_k + \mathbf{t})\|^2$$ which has a closed-form solution via SVD of the cross-covariance (Umeyama's method). Three non-collinear points suffice; in practice use many, well spread. Caveat: a single global Sim(3) fixes scale at the alignment points but cannot fix drift or bending in between. To constrain those, the markers must enter the bundle adjustment as constraints (GCPs), not just a post-hoc alignment.

    2.1.7Ground control points, checkpoints, accuracy vs precision, reprojection vs true error#

    • Ground control points (GCPs) are surveyed points whose coordinates are used inside the adjustment to fix datum and scale (and reduce drift).
    • Checkpoints (CPs) are surveyed points that are withheld from the adjustment and used only for evaluation. Accuracy must be reported on checkpoints; reporting it on GCPs is like reporting training loss. For this project: printed markers, a subset used as GCPs/scale bars, the rest as checkpoints, with tape/laser distances as ground truth.
    • Accuracy = closeness to the true value (bias + spread). Precision = repeatability/spread (e.g. the standard deviation BA reports from its covariance). A reconstruction can be extremely precise and wrong: consistently 2% too large, or consistently folded.
    • Reprojection error (mean pixel distance between observed and projected features) measures self-consistency of the model with the observations it was fit to. It does not measure accuracy. A map folded by a false loop closure, or uniformly mis-scaled, can have sub-pixel reprojection error. True error requires external ground truth: checkpoint RMSE, distance errors between markers, or comparison with a laser scan.
    • Checkpoint RMSE $=\sqrt{\tfrac1N\sum_k\|\hat{\mathbf{q}}_k - \mathbf{q}_k\|^2}$, usually also reported per axis, plus relative distance error $|\hat d - d|/d$ for marker pairs (our ≤1% target).

    2.2The classic photogrammetry and 3D reconstruction pipeline#

    The canonical pipeline is: images → features → matches → sparse SfM (poses + sparse points) → dense MVS (depth maps) → fusion → mesh → texture. Neural methods (§2.3–2.4) mostly replace the last three stages and still consume SfM poses. Bad poses poison everything downstream.

    2.2.1Features and matching#

    Hand-crafted features.

    • SIFT [120] detects blobs in a difference-of-Gaussians scale space and describes each with a 128-D histogram of gradient orientations, normalised for rotation and scale. Still COLMAP's default and still very competitive for accuracy.
    • ORB [168] is a fast binary descriptor (oriented FAST corners + rotated BRIEF), compared with Hamming distance. It is the backbone of ORB-SLAM. Fast but less distinctive than SIFT.

    Learned features and matchers.

    • SuperPoint [39]: a CNN that jointly detects keypoints and describes them, trained self-supervised via synthetic shapes and homographic adaptation.
    • SuperGlue [173] and its faster successor LightGlue [117] are attention-based matchers: given two sets of keypoints, they reason jointly about which match, using context. LightGlue adapts its depth to pair difficulty. Code and weights are Apache-2.0; the README notes SuperPoint itself has a more restrictive (non-commercial) licence.
    • Detector-free / dense matchers: LoFTR [191] matches on a coarse transformer feature grid and refines; it works where keypoint detectors find nothing (low texture). RoMa [52] regresses a dense warp with DINOv2 features and is among the most robust matchers; code MIT (DINOv2 Apache-2.0).
    • MASt3R [111] grounds matching in 3D: it predicts dense 3D pointmaps and local descriptors for an image pair, yielding matches that are robust to large viewpoint change. Licence CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 (non-commercial).
    • COLMAP 4.2.0 added LoMa learned extraction/matching via ONNX [23].

    Vocabulary trees and loop detection. Exhaustive matching is $O(N^2)$ pairs. Image retrieval quantises descriptors into "visual words" with a hierarchical k-means vocabulary tree [142] and ranks candidate pairs by TF-IDF similarity; learned global descriptors (NetVLAD and successors) do the same with a CNN. For video, sequential matching (each frame with its next $k$ neighbours) plus retrieval-based loop detection is standard. Retrieval proposes pairs; geometric verification (RANSAC on $\mathbf{E}$/$\mathbf{F}$) accepts them.

    Why it matters here. In a parking garage, retrieval will happily propose "bay 12 ↔ bay 31" because they look the same, and geometric verification may accept it. Dim light also reduces SIFT keypoint counts and pushes towards learned matchers, but learned matchers are better at matching repeated structure, which can make false loop closures more likely, not less. Robust pipelines combine temporal priors (sequential video), conservative loop acceptance, and checks of the pose graph.

    2.2.2Structure from Motion (SfM)#

    SfM recovers camera poses (and often intrinsics) plus a sparse point cloud from unordered or ordered images.

    Incremental SfM (Bundler [183] → VisualSFM [214] → COLMAP [175]): pick a well-conditioned initial pair, triangulate, then repeatedly register the next best image with PnP, triangulate new points, and run local/global BA. Robust and accurate, but slow for large sets and prone to drift and to order-dependent failures; errors early on are baked in.

    Global SfM: estimate all relative poses first, then solve for all absolute orientations at once (rotation averaging, robustly over the view graph), then all positions (translation averaging, harder because pairwise translations have unknown scale), then one BA. Faster and less drift-prone, historically less robust. GLOMAP [156] replaced translation averaging with a joint global positioning of cameras and points and reported accuracy comparable to or better than COLMAP's incremental mapper at much higher speed. Since COLMAP 4.0.0, GLOMAP is integrated into COLMAP as the global mapper, and 4.2.0 added multi-component support (disconnected view-graph components are reconstructed separately) [23].

    Hierarchical SfM partitions the view graph into clusters, reconstructs each, and merges them. Useful at large scale; merges are another place where repetitive scenes can mis-register.

    Rigs and multi-camera SfM. A rig is several cameras with fixed relative poses (sensor_from_rig). Modelling the rig reduces unknowns (one pose per frame, not per camera) and adds strong constraints. COLMAP has first-class rig support, and its 360° workflow treats virtual perspective views rendered from each panorama as a rig with known extrinsics [24].

    2.2.3SLAM versus SfM#

    Visual SLAM (Simultaneous Localisation and Mapping) processes a video stream causally and in real time: track the current frame against a local map, insert keyframes, run local BA, detect loops, and correct drift with pose-graph optimisation or global BA. ORB-SLAM3 [13] supports monocular, stereo, RGB-D, visual-inertial and fisheye cameras, multi-map "atlas" operation and place recognition; licence GPLv3.

    SfM SLAM
    Input unordered or ordered images, offline video stream, online
    Goal max accuracy real-time, bounded latency
    Optimisation (repeated) full BA local BA + pose graph / occasional full BA
    Typical failure registration gaps, slow tracking loss under fast motion/low texture, drift

    Drift is the accumulation of small relative-pose errors; it grows with trajectory length. Loop closure recognises a revisited place and adds a constraint that redistributes accumulated error around the loop. It is powerful and dangerous: one false loop closure in a repetitive garage glues two different bays together, "folding" the map, and subsequent optimisation will happily make it self-consistent. Visual-inertial odometry fuses IMU data, which gives metric scale, gravity direction and robustness to fast rotation. For the Avata 360, Ch. 1 finds high-rate fused attitude quaternions in the .OSV metadata (usable as gravity and rotation priors) but no logged metric accelerations or drone VIO, so visual-inertial scale is not available.

    For this project: SLAM-style sequential processing is a good fit for FPV video (strong temporal priors, fewer spurious long-range pairs), while offline SfM/BA is needed for the final accuracy.

    2.2.4Dense reconstruction#

    SfM gives poses and a sparse cloud. Multi-view stereo (MVS) estimates a depth (and normal) for every pixel.

    • Plane-sweep stereo: for each candidate depth plane, warp neighbouring images into the reference view and compute photo-consistency (e.g. NCC); pick the best depth per pixel. Simple and GPU-friendly; basis of many learned MVS networks (cost volumes).
    • PatchMatch MVS: initialise per-pixel depth/normal hypotheses randomly, then propagate good hypotheses to neighbours and refine by random perturbation, exploiting spatial coherence. COLMAP's dense stereo adds pixel-wise view selection and geometric consistency [174]; OpenMVS and ACMM-family methods are other implementations.
    • Depth fusion: merge per-view depth maps into one point cloud, keeping points consistent across views (COLMAP's stereo_fusion).
    • TSDF fusion [34]: accumulate a truncated signed distance to the surface in a voxel grid, averaging over views; extract the zero level set with marching cubes. Common for RGB-D and for extracting meshes from rendered depths (2DGS, GOF use variants).
    • Meshing: Screened Poisson [99] fits an implicit indicator function to oriented points (watertight, smooth, may hallucinate surfaces in holes); Delaunay/graph-cut meshing (OpenMVS) labels tetrahedra inside/outside using visibility; advancing-front grows a surface over the points (COLMAP 4.1.0 added an advancing-front mesher [23]).
    • Texturing: project images onto mesh faces, choose the best view per face, blend seams.

    Tools. COLMAP (BSD, SfM + MVS + meshing) [175]; OpenMVS [145] (AGPL-3.0; densify, mesh, refine, texture); Meshroom/AliceVision (MPL-2.0) [69]; OpenDroneMap/ODM [143] (AGPL-3.0; aerial-mapping oriented, GNSS-centric). Commercial reference points: Agisoft Metashape and RealityScan (formerly RealityCapture), both widely used in surveying and known for strong GCP workflows. (Assessment, not measured here.)

    Why it matters here. Classical MVS relies on photo-consistency, which fails on textureless concrete (no signal), specular car paint and wet floors (view-dependent appearance), and moving objects. Expect holes on floors/ceilings and noise on cars. Planar priors (garages are mostly planes) are therefore valuable. Classical MVS remains the accuracy reference for textured surfaces.

    2.2.5Evaluation metrics#

    • Accuracy (precision, in the benchmark sense): for each reconstructed point, distance to the nearest ground-truth point; report mean/median or the fraction below a threshold $\tau$.
    • Completeness (recall): for each ground-truth point, distance to the nearest reconstructed point.
    • F-score: harmonic mean of precision and recall at threshold $\tau$, $F = 2PR/(P+R)$. Popularised for full scenes by Tanks and Temples [106].
    • Chamfer distance: the mean of the two directed nearest-neighbour distances, $\mathrm{CD} = \tfrac12\big(\tfrac{1}{|A|}\sum_{a}\min_b\|a-b\| + \tfrac{1}{|B|}\sum_{b}\min_a\|a-b\|\big)$ (some papers use squared distances; check the definition). DTU reports this.
    • ATE / RPE for trajectories [190]: ATE aligns the estimated trajectory to ground truth (rigidly, or by Sim(3) for monocular) and reports RMSE of position differences, which captures global consistency; RPE measures the error of relative motion over a fixed interval $\Delta$, which captures local drift.
    • Checkpoint RMSE and marker-distance error (§2.1.7): the survey-style metrics that match this project's acceptance criterion.
    • Rendering metrics (for the visualisation goal only): PSNR, SSIM, LPIPS on held-out views. These say nothing about metric accuracy.

    2.3Neural and learned reconstruction#

    2.3.1NeRF and the volume rendering equation#

    NeRF [128] represents a scene as an MLP $F_\Theta(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{d}) \mapsto (\sigma, \mathbf{c})$: volume density $\sigma$ at position $\mathbf{x}$ and view-dependent colour $\mathbf{c}$ for viewing direction $\mathbf{d}$. A pixel's colour is the expected colour along its camera ray $\mathbf{r}(t) = \mathbf{o} + t\mathbf{d}$: $$C(\mathbf{r}) = \int_{t_n}^{t_f} T(t)\,\sigma(\mathbf{r}(t))\,\mathbf{c}(\mathbf{r}(t),\mathbf{d})\,dt, \qquad T(t) = \exp\!\Big(-\!\int_{t_n}^{t}\sigma(\mathbf{r}(s))\,ds\Big).$$ $T(t)$ is the transmittance: the probability that light travels from $t_n$ to $t$ unblocked. Discretised with samples $t_i$ and spacings $\delta_i$: $$\hat C = \sum_i T_i\,\alpha_i\,\mathbf{c}_i,\quad \alpha_i = 1 - e^{-\sigma_i\delta_i},\quad T_i = \prod_{j<i}(1-\alpha_j).$$ This alpha-compositing form is exactly what 3DGS also uses, with Gaussians instead of ray samples. The whole thing is differentiable, so $\Theta$ is fitted by gradient descent on a photometric loss against the training images, given known camera poses (from COLMAP).

    Positional encoding. MLPs are biased towards low frequencies ("spectral bias"). NeRF maps each coordinate through $\gamma(p) = (\sin 2^0\pi p, \cos 2^0\pi p, \dots, \sin 2^{L-1}\pi p, \cos 2^{L-1}\pi p)$ so the network can represent fine detail; Tancik et al. later analysed this as Fourier features [195].

    Mip-NeRF 360 [10]. Important for vocabulary: "360" here means unbounded scenes captured by a camera orbiting a central object (with background extending to infinity), not panoramic/360° input images. It adds (i) a non-linear scene contraction that maps unbounded space into a bounded ball, (ii) online distillation with a small proposal MLP to place samples, and (iii) a distortion regulariser that suppresses floaters. It builds on mip-NeRF's cone tracing with integrated positional encoding, which is a principled anti-aliasing scheme. Its "Mip-NeRF 360 dataset" became the standard 3DGS benchmark.

    Instant-NGP [137] replaced most of the MLP with a multiresolution hash encoding: $L$ grids of increasing resolution, each storing trainable feature vectors in a hash table of size $T$; collisions are tolerated and resolved implicitly by training. Training dropped from hours to seconds-to-minutes. Licence: NVIDIA source-code licence (non-commercial use; commercial via NVIDIA).

    Zip-NeRF [11] combines mip-NeRF 360's anti-aliasing with hash-grid speed, using multisampling to approximate cone integrals over the grid. It was long the quality reference for NeRF on unbounded scenes.

    Why it matters here. NeRFs give photoreal views but geometry is a by-product of density, poorly constrained in textureless or specular regions. They are training-time and memory heavy relative to 3DGS. For this project they are mainly vocabulary and background for neural surfaces.

    2.3.2Neural implicit surfaces: NeuS, VolSDF, Neuralangelo#

    Density fields do not define a crisp surface. Neural surface methods instead learn a signed distance function (SDF) $f(\mathbf{x})$ and convert it to density for volume rendering, so the surface is the zero level set and can be extracted with marching cubes.

    • NeuS [206] derives an unbiased SDF→opacity conversion using a logistic density $\phi_s(f)$ whose sharpness $s$ is learned, so that the rendering weight peaks exactly at the surface.
    • VolSDF [221] defines density as a scaled Laplace CDF of the negative SDF, $\sigma(\mathbf{x}) = \alpha\,\Psi_\beta(-f(\mathbf{x}))$, and derives an error bound to guide sampling.
    • Neuralangelo [112] combines multiresolution hash grids with numerical gradients and coarse-to-fine optimisation to recover high-detail surfaces at large scale. Licence: NVIDIA source-code licence (non-commercial).
    • Eikonal loss $\mathbb{E}\,(\|\nabla f\|-1)^2$ keeps $f$ a valid distance field in all of these.

    Trade-off: smooth, watertight geometry with good hole-filling on textureless areas; slow training (hours to days), and smoothness priors can erase thin structures. IM360 (shared context) is an example that pairs spherical SfM with neural surface reconstruction for large indoor 360° scenes [97].

    2.3.3Feed-forward 3D: DUSt3R, MASt3R, VGGT and successors#

    A new family of large networks predicts 3D structure from images in one forward pass, without per-scene optimisation or known intrinsics.

    • DUSt3R [210] (CVPR 2024): given two images, a ViT predicts a dense pointmap for each, i.e. a 3D point per pixel, both expressed in the first camera's frame, plus confidence. Relative pose, intrinsics and depth fall out of the pointmaps. For more than two views, pairwise predictions are fused by a global alignment optimisation. It does not use SuperGlue or any keypoint matcher. Code and checkpoints: CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 (non-commercial), with training-data licence caveats.
    • MASt3R [111] (ECCV 2024): adds a dense local-feature head to DUSt3R for accurate, reciprocal matching. MASt3R-SfM [51] (3DV 2025) builds a full SfM pipeline on it. CC BY-NC-SA 4.0.
    • VGGT [212] (CVPR 2025): a 1B-parameter transformer that takes many views at once and outputs camera parameters, depth maps, pointmaps and point tracks. Since July 2025 the code and a separate commercial checkpoint permit commercial use (excluding military); the original checkpoint remains non-commercial.
    • π³ [211]: removes the reference-view dependence with a permutation-equivariant architecture.
    • MapAnything [101] (3DV 2026): a feed-forward model that predicts metric 3D geometry and can accept optional inputs such as intrinsics, poses or depth; an Apache-2.0 model variant is offered.

    What they predict and their limits. They predict plausible geometry from learned priors. Strengths: robustness where classical matching fails (low texture, wide baselines), excellent initialisation, very fast. Limits for this project: (1) working resolution is typically ~512 px on the long side, far below 8K; (2) memory grows with the number of views, so long videos need chunking (VGGT-Long-style approaches); (3) accuracy is generally below BA-refined SfM on well-textured scenes, so the outputs are best used as an initialisation or matcher and then refined with BA; (4) "metric" predictions are learned scale estimates, not survey-grade, and must still be checked against markers; (5) most assume pinhole input, so equirectangular frames need to be split into perspective views first; (6) several have non-commercial licences.

    2.43D Gaussian Splatting in depth#

    3DGS [102] (Kerbl, Kopanas, Leimkühler, Drettakis; ACM TOG 42(4), SIGGRAPH 2023) replaced NeRF's implicit field with an explicit set of millions of 3D Gaussians, rendered by fast rasterisation instead of ray marching. Training takes minutes to tens of minutes; rendering runs in real time (≥30 fps at 1080p per the paper).

    2.4.1Representation#

    Each Gaussian $k$ has:

    • mean $\boldsymbol{\mu}_k\in\mathbb{R}^3$;
    • covariance $\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_k = \mathbf{R}_k\mathbf{S}_k\mathbf{S}_k^\top\mathbf{R}_k^\top$, parameterised by a scale vector $\mathbf{s}_k$ (stored as log-scales; $\mathbf{S}=\mathrm{diag}(\mathbf{s})$) and a unit quaternion $\mathbf{q}_k$ ($\to\mathbf{R}_k$). This factorisation keeps $\boldsymbol\Sigma$ positive semi-definite during gradient descent, which optimising $\boldsymbol\Sigma$ directly would not;
    • opacity $o_k\in(0,1)$ (stored as a logit);
    • colour as spherical-harmonic (SH) coefficients, degree 3 by default (16 coefficients per channel, 48 floats), so colour can vary with viewing direction (a cheap model of specularity).

    The density is $G_k(\mathbf{x}) = \exp\!\big(-\tfrac12(\mathbf{x}-\boldsymbol\mu_k)^\top\boldsymbol\Sigma_k^{-1}(\mathbf{x}-\boldsymbol\mu_k)\big)$.

    2.4.2EWA projection, tile rasterisation and alpha compositing#

    Projection. Following EWA splatting [232], a 3D Gaussian under a locally affine approximation of the projection becomes a 2D Gaussian with covariance $$\boldsymbol\Sigma'_k = \mathbf{J}\,\mathbf{W}\,\boldsymbol\Sigma_k\,\mathbf{W}^\top\mathbf{J}^\top,$$ where $\mathbf{W}$ is the world-to-camera rotation and $\mathbf{J}$ the Jacobian of the perspective projection at the Gaussian's centre (keeping the upper-left $2\times2$). This linearisation is exact only for pinhole projection near the centre; it degrades for wide-angle, fisheye and spherical cameras and cannot model rolling shutter. That limitation motivates 3DGUT/3DGRT (§2.4.4).

    Tile-based rasterisation. The screen is split into 16×16-pixel tiles. Each projected Gaussian (culled to its ~3σ extent) is duplicated for every tile it overlaps, with a key combining tile ID and depth; one GPU radix sort orders everything; each tile then walks its depth-sorted list front to back.

    Alpha compositing. For pixel $\mathbf{p}$, $$C(\mathbf{p}) = \sum_{k\in\mathcal{N}} \mathbf{c}_k\,\alpha_k \prod_{j<k}(1-\alpha_j),\qquad \alpha_k = o_k\exp\!\big(-\tfrac12(\mathbf{p}-\boldsymbol\mu'_k)^\top\boldsymbol\Sigma'^{-1}_k(\mathbf{p}-\boldsymbol\mu'_k)\big).$$ Same form as NeRF's discrete volume rendering, with Gaussians replacing ray samples. Processing stops when accumulated opacity saturates. Sorting is per Gaussian centre, not per pixel, which is an approximation and causes popping when ordering changes with viewpoint.

    Differentiability. The backward pass re-traverses the sorted lists back to front, propagating gradients to $\boldsymbol\mu, \mathbf{s}, \mathbf{q}, o$ and SH coefficients. Everything is custom CUDA.

    2.4.3Optimisation#

    • Initialisation: Gaussians are created at the SfM sparse points, with colour from the points and scale from nearest-neighbour distances. 3DGS therefore inherits COLMAP's poses and its sparse coverage. Poor SfM in textureless regions means few seeds there.
    • Loss: $\mathcal{L} = (1-\lambda)\,\mathcal{L}_1 + \lambda\,\mathcal{L}_{\text{D-SSIM}}$ with $\lambda = 0.2$.
    • Adaptive density control (every 100 iterations from iteration 500 to 15,000 by default in the reference code [102]): Gaussians whose view-space positional gradient exceeds a threshold (default 0.0002) are densified. Clone small ones (under-reconstruction: copy and move along the gradient). Split large ones (over-reconstruction: replace with two smaller samples drawn from the original). Prune Gaussians with very low opacity or excessive size.
    • Opacity reset: periodically set all opacities to a small value so that unnecessary Gaussians (especially floaters near cameras) can be pruned.
    • Schedule: 30,000 iterations by default; the reference README asks for 24 GB VRAM to train to paper quality, with options for smaller GPUs [102]. gsplat reports reproducing the original with up to 4× less GPU memory [224], which is directly relevant to the 16 GB budget.
    • Alternative densification: MCMC-style relocation (available in gsplat) treats Gaussians as samples and caps their number, which is useful for memory control.

    Common failure modes.

    • Floaters: semi-transparent blobs in free space that explain view-specific effects (exposure changes, reflections, moving objects) for a few training views.
    • Needles/spikes: highly anisotropic Gaussians that look fine from training views but streak from novel ones.
    • Popping: sudden changes when sort order flips.
    • Overfitting to training views: good PSNR on training views but artefacts off-trajectory. FPV footage samples a narrow corridor of viewpoints, which makes this likely.
    • Specular/reflective surfaces: SH colour cannot represent mirror-like reflections, so 3DGS often "paints" a reflected scene behind the surface, which wrecks geometry on car paint, glass and wet floors.
    Widget

    2D Gaussian splatting toy

    iterations
    0
    Gaussians
    0
    PSNR
    –

    What it teaches: an image as a sum of anisotropic Gaussians (mean, covariance, colour) optimised by gradient descent (Adam) on a photometric loss, with adaptive density control that clones Gaussians with large positional gradients and splits large ones, under a cap like the MCMC variant's. Simplified: 2D, additive blending without depth ordering. Project link: a good photometric fit says nothing about whether the Gaussians sit on true surfaces, which is why the splat is anchored to the metric mesh rather than used as the measurement (§2.4.5, §5.5).

    2.4.4The extension landscape (grouped)#

    Anti-aliasing. Mip-Splatting [226] adds a 3D smoothing filter (bounded by the maximal sampling rate of the training views) and a 2D Mip filter to remove aliasing/dilation artefacts when zooming or changing resolution.

    Surfaces and geometry.

    • SuGaR [74]: regularises Gaussians to align with the surface, extracts a mesh (Poisson), optionally binds Gaussians to the mesh for joint refinement. CVPR 2024.
    • 2DGS [88] (SIGGRAPH 2024): replaces 3D ellipsoids with oriented 2D disks (surfels) with ray–splat intersection, adding depth-distortion and normal-consistency losses; meshes via TSDF fusion of rendered depth. Consistently better geometry than 3DGS, slightly lower rendering quality.
    • GOF (Gaussian Opacity Fields) [225] (ACM TOG 2024): defines a view-independent opacity field from the Gaussians and extracts its level set directly (via a tetrahedral grid), suitable for unbounded scenes.
    • PGSR [16]: flattens Gaussians into planes, renders unbiased depth and normals, and adds single- and multi-view geometric consistency plus exposure compensation. Particularly interesting for planar-dominated scenes like parking garages. Licence: research/non-profit only.
    • RaDe-GS [229]: rasterises depth and normal maps for general Gaussians efficiently; the README says it is accepted to ACM TOG and now integrates PGSR's multi-view regularisation.
    • Licence caveat: SuGaR, 2DGS, GOF, Mip-Splatting and RaDe-GS ship the Inria/MPII Gaussian-Splatting licence (non-commercial research and evaluation) because they derive from the reference code.

    Appearance and in-the-wild. Real captures have exposure, white-balance and vignetting changes; vanilla 3DGS does not handle these, it bakes them into floaters or colour errors. Remedies: per-image appearance embeddings (NeRF-W style), per-image affine colour transforms, bilateral grids [208] (integrated in gsplat), and the exposure-compensation option added to the INRIA code in its October 2024 update [102]. WildGaussians [107] (NeurIPS 2024) adds DINO-feature-based appearance and uncertainty modelling to handle transient occluders such as people and cars. For flickering artificial light, appearance modelling is not optional.

    Camera models: fisheye, 360°, rolling shutter.

    • 3DGRT [132] (ACM TOG / SIGGRAPH Asia 2024) ray-traces Gaussian particles with hardware ray tracing, so any camera model and secondary effects are possible, at higher cost.
    • 3DGUT [215] (CVPR 2025) keeps rasterisation but replaces the EWA linearisation with an unscented transform: project sigma points of each Gaussian through the exact nonlinear camera model (including distortion and time-dependent rolling-shutter effects) and refit a 2D Gaussian. Code Apache-2.0 (3dgrut), and integrated into gsplat.
    • Fisheye-GS adapts the projection Jacobian to the equidistant fisheye model; 3DGUT's README follows its ScanNet++ preprocessing.
    • Omnidirectional splatting: OmniGS [113] (WACV 2025) and ODGS [109] (NeurIPS 2024; MIT licence) rasterise Gaussians directly into equirectangular images.
    • Rolling shutter and motion blur: Gaussian Splatting on the Move [176] (ECCV 2024) models both in a differentiable renderer using velocity estimates, and refines poses.

    Scale and compression. Hierarchical 3DGS [103] (ACM TOG 2024) builds a level-of-detail hierarchy and trains large scenes in chunks, which is the natural route for a whole garage on 16 GB. A large compression literature (quantisation, pruning, SH distillation, anchor-based scaffolds) shrinks models by 10–50×; see the surveys [17].

    Tooling.

    • INRIA reference code [102]: canonical, non-commercial licence, CUDA.
    • gsplat [224] (Apache-2.0; JMLR 2025): a library of CUDA kernels with Python bindings; supports 3DGUT, fisheye/FTheta camera models, MCMC densification, bilateral grids and PPISP appearance compensation (per its README news).
    • nerfstudio [196] (Apache-2.0): a modular framework; its splatfacto method is built on gsplat and accepts COLMAP data.

    2.4.5Why 3DGS geometry is not automatically metric or accurate#

    1. Scale and frame are inherited from SfM. 3DGS has no notion of metres; its scale is whatever the input poses had. If SfM drifted or folded, the splat faithfully reproduces that error.
    2. The objective is photometric. The loss rewards matching training pixels, not placing Gaussians on surfaces. Any arrangement that renders correctly from the training views is a valid minimum. Reflections get painted behind surfaces, textureless walls get arbitrary depth, and floaters fill free space.
    3. There is no single surface. Gaussians are semi-transparent volumes; "depth" must be defined (expected depth, median depth, opacity level set), and different definitions disagree by centimetres. 2DGS/GOF/PGSR/RaDe-GS exist to address exactly this.
    4. Coverage bias. FPV trajectories see floors and ceilings at grazing angles; geometry there is weakly constrained.
    5. Evaluation mismatch. High PSNR on held-out views does not imply geometric accuracy.

    Practical consequence for this project: treat SfM + BA (with GCPs) as the metric backbone, MVS or a geometry-regularised splatting method as the dense-geometry source validated on checkpoints, and vanilla 3DGS as the visualisation layer.

    2.5Spherical / 360° specifics#

    2.5.1Equirectangular and cubemap representations#

    • Equirectangular (ERP): longitude/latitude grid (§2.1.2). Easy to store and stream; heavy, non-uniform distortion: a pixel at latitude $\theta$ covers a solid angle proportional to $\cos\theta$, so the poles are hugely oversampled and objects there are stretched horizontally. Local features computed on ERP pixels are not rotation/scale-invariant in the usual sense.
    • Cubemap: six 90°-FoV pinhole images on the faces of a cube centred at the camera. Each face is an ordinary perspective image, so all classical tools work, at the cost of seams between faces and features that straddle edges. Variants render overlapping views (e.g. 8–12 views with >90° FoV, or RIGOR's four-view virtual rig per gravity-aligned panorama [166]) to keep features away from edges.
    • Dual-fisheye (raw): the two native lens images. They avoid stitching artefacts and are a central model per lens; they need a two-camera rig model with a small baseline and fisheye intrinsics. Ch. 1 shows that the Avata 360's .OSV file does contain both raw 3840×3840 fisheye streams, and Ch. 4 builds its primary pipeline on them.

    The mapping ERP → cubemap face is: for each face pixel, form the ray $\mathbf{K}_{face}^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}$, rotate it by the face rotation, convert to $(\phi,\theta)$, and sample the ERP with bilinear/bicubic interpolation.

    Widget

    Equirectangular ↔ cubemap mapper

    longitude φ, latitude θ
    8K ERP pixel (7680×3840)
    cube face
    face pixel (1920² face)
    ERP horizontal oversampling 1/cos θ

    Arrow keys move the probe when the map has focus. Dashed red line: on this drone the stitch seam lies on the horizon (latitude 0).

    What it teaches: ERP maps longitude/latitude linearly, so a pixel at latitude θ covers a solid angle proportional to cos θ: the poles are hugely oversampled. A cubemap face is an ordinary perspective image; the mapping uses the report's convention $u = \tfrac{W}{2\pi}(\phi+\pi)$, $v = \tfrac{H}{\pi}(\tfrac{\pi}{2}-\theta)$. Project link: on the Avata 360 the lens centres are the zenith and nadir (ERP's stretched rows) and the seam is the equator, the opposite of the usual "seam at the back" layout (§1.2.3, §2.5.1). The 1920 px face is simply 7680/4 for illustration.

    2.5.2Spherical epipolar geometry#

    With bearing vectors the essential-matrix constraint holds unchanged: $\mathbf{b}_2^\top[\mathbf{t}]_\times\mathbf{R}\,\mathbf{b}_1 = 0$. Geometrically, all rays from both centres to a 3D point lie in the epipolar plane, and that plane cuts each sphere in a great circle. Errors must be measured as angles on the sphere (or in a local tangent plane), not in ERP pixels, because a pixel near the pole subtends a much smaller angle than one at the equator. Benefits of the full sphere: wide field of view makes rotation estimation very well conditioned and reduces the forward-motion ambiguity typical of narrow cameras. Costs: lower angular resolution per pixel (8K ERP spreads 7,680 pixels over 360°, roughly 21 px/degree, versus ~40–60 px/degree for a typical 4K perspective video with 70–90° FoV), and seam/stitching artefacts.

    2.5.3Why perspective rigs often beat native spherical models in current SfM tools#

    COLMAP 4.1.0's changelog says the new spherical (equirectangular) camera models enable native reconstruction of 360° images but are "generally faster but less accurate than rendering perspective views", as in its panorama_sfm example [23]. Its docs describe the recommended workflow: render virtual pinhole images (similar to a cubemap) and treat them as a rig with known extrinsics and intrinsics, which makes reconstruction more robust [24]. Plausible reasons (assessment):

    1. Feature detectors and descriptors (SIFT, SuperPoint, …) are designed and trained for perspective images; ERP distortion changes local appearance with latitude.
    2. Pixel-space residuals in ERP are anisotropic and latitude-dependent unless the solver works in angular/tangent-plane errors.
    3. Rig constraints with known relative poses are strong regularisers, and rig BA is mature.
    4. Learned matchers and MVS (and feed-forward models) assume pinhole input.

    The ORBIT benchmark [150] (accepted at CVPR 2026; arXiv version titled ORBIT++) tracks web-sourced 360° videos to obtain reference trajectories, then crops them into challenging perspective clips; COLMAP and other state-of-the-art SfM methods still struggle on those clips. IM360 [97] integrates the spherical camera model into every core SfM component, with dense matching designed for 360° images, for large indoor scenes. RIGOR [166] takes the rig route: it treats each gravity-aligned panorama as a four-view virtual rig fed to a frozen feed-forward perspective backbone, uses the rig to detect inconsistent predictions and verify loop closures, and corrects drift with a Sim(3) pose graph.

    2.5.4360° datasets#

    • Matterport3D [14] (3DV 2017): RGB-D data captured throughout 90 properties with a Matterport Pro camera (colour and depth images, camera poses, textured meshes, floor plans, semantics). Access requires signing terms of use.
    • Stanford 2D-3D-Semantics (2D-3D-S) [7]: indoor office areas with equirectangular RGB, depth, normals and semantics; licence agreement required.
    • 360Loc [89] (CVPR 2024): omnidirectional visual-localisation benchmark with 360° mapping images and cross-device queries (360°, pinhole and several fisheye variants generated from the 360° images) (scenes include one named atrium). Useful for testing 360° localisation pipelines.
    • ORBIT [150] (CVPR 2026): pose-estimation benchmark of challenging clips cropped from web 360° videos, with reference trajectories from panoramic rig-based SfM.
    • Others to look at, not verified here: Structured3D (synthetic panoramas), 360VO/360 SLAM datasets, TartanAir panorama sequences (COLMAP 4.2.0 notes mention TartanAir panoramas in its reconstruction benchmarks [23]). UNVERIFIED beyond that note.

    None of these is a dim, repetitive underground garage captured by an FPV drone; the project will likely need its own small benchmark with surveyed markers.

Resources marked † were not opened in this session (network limits); they are canonical and the reader should confirm current URLs.
